The coup failed in Madrid, Barcelona, Valencia, Bilbao and much of the industrial north and east, but succeeded in Spanish Morocco, the Canaries, most of the Balearics, Galicia, Old Castile, Navarre and pockets of western Andalusia. What was meant to be a quick seizure of power became a war of almost three years. It was at once a class war, a clash over religion and the Church, a struggle between centralism and regional autonomy (Catalonia, the Basque Country), and an early battleground of the wider European confrontation between fascism, communism and liberal democracy."},{"h":"The Republic and its defenders","t":"The Republican (loyalist) side was a coalition: the government of the Republic led in turn by José Giral, Francisco Largo Caballero and Juan Negrín; the left Republicans of President Manuel Azaña; the Socialists (PSOE) and their union UGT; the Communists (PCE), whose influence grew with Soviet aid; the anarcho-syndicalist CNT-FAI; the anti-Stalinist Marxist POUM; and the autonomous governments of Catalonia and the Basque Country. In summer 1936 party and union militias did most of the fighting; from late 1936 they were folded into the Ejército Popular (People's Army). Foreign volunteers of the International Brigades and Soviet tanks, aircraft and advisers — paid for with the Bank of Spain's gold reserve — were the Republic's main outside support. Mexico also sent arms."},{"h":"The rebels: 'Nationalists' or 'sublevados'","t":"The rebels called themselves nacionales; Republicans called them facciosos or rebeldes, and historians often use sublevados (the risen). Their core was the army, above all the professional Army of Africa — the Spanish Legion and Moroccan Regulares — commanded by Franco, with General Emilio Mola directing the plot from Pamplona. Civilian supporters included the fascist Falange, the Carlist Requetés of Navarre, monarchists and people and networks of the Catholic right. CEDA itself was breaking up by 1936: its leader Gil Robles and others backed the conspirators, while many of its younger members moved into the Falange. In April 1937 Franco merged Falange and Carlists into a single party, FET y de las JONS. Nazi Germany sent the Condor Legion (air and armour, with rotating crews) and Fascist Italy the Corpo Truppe Volontarie (CTV) and the Aviazione Legionaria; Portugal gave logistic support and volunteers."},{"h":"How the war ran","t":"In the first summer rebel columns from the south advanced up the Tagus towards Madrid while Mola's northern forces fought for the Guadarrama passes. Madrid held in November 1936, and battles around it — Jarama, Guadalajara, Brunete — failed to break the stalemate. Franco turned north: Bilbao fell in June 1937, Santander in August and Asturias in October, giving the rebels the industrial north. Republican offensives at Belchite and Teruel won ground but could not keep it. In spring 1938 the Nationalists reached the Mediterranean at Vinaròs, cutting the Republic in two. The Republic's last great offensive, across the Ebro (July–November 1938), bled its army white. Catalonia fell in January–February 1939."},{"h":"How it ended","t":"After Barcelona fell on 26 January 1939, around half a million people fled across the Pyrenees into France in the Retirada. Britain and France recognised Franco's government on 27 February and President Azaña resigned in exile. In the remaining central zone, Prime Minister Negrín wanted to resist in the hope that a European war would change the balance, but on 5 March Colonel Segismundo Casado seized power in Madrid to negotiate a surrender, leading to fighting among Republicans. Franco accepted only unconditional surrender. His troops entered Madrid on 28 March; thousands of Republicans waited in vain for ships at Alicante. Franco's final war communiqué was signed at Burgos on 1 April 1939. Formal war ended; repression, prison camps and exile did not."},{"h":"The cost","t":"The total death toll is still debated; a commonly cited overall figure is around half a million, but historians' estimates vary widely. Beyond combat deaths, both zones saw mass killing behind the lines. For the Nationalist zone during the war, estimates range from under 50,000 to 200,000 (Casanova c. 100,000; Thomas 75,000), with tens of thousands more executed after 1939. For the Republican zone, most historians place the figure around 38,000–55,000 (Beevor 38,000; Payne and Juliá 50,000; Thomas and Preston 55,000), including 6,832 clergy. Cities such as Madrid, Barcelona, Gernika, Valencia and Almería were bombed from the air. Hundreds of thousands went into exile; many never returned."},{"h":"Memory: silence, pact and law","t":"Under Franco (to 1975) only the victors' dead were honoured; the Valley of the Fallen (now Valle de Cuelgamuros) became the regime's great monument. The transition to democracy rested on an amnesty (1977) and an informal 'pact of forgetting'. From 2000 civil associations began exhuming mass graves. The Law of Historical Memory (Ley 52/2007, 26 December 2007) recognised victims of both sides, supported exhumations and ordered the removal of Francoist symbols. Franco's remains were moved from Cuelgamuros on 24 October 2019. The Democratic Memory Law (Ley 20/2022, in force 21 October 2022) replaced it: the state takes charge of exhumations and a DNA bank, Francoist court sentences are declared void, and the official name of the Valle de los Caídos was changed to Valle de Cuelgamuros, now a Place of Democratic Memory. The subject remains politically divisive, and several regions have their own memory laws."},{"h":"Visiting today","t":"Much of the war's landscape survives: trench lines in the Sierra de Guadarrama, Jarama and Aragon; ruined villages kept as memorials at Belchite and Corbera d'Ebre; bunkers of Bilbao's Iron Belt; air-raid shelters in Barcelona, Valencia, Cartagena and Almería; and mass-grave memorials in every region. Many sites are on open hillside or farmland — respect private land, do not dig or collect metal (it is illegal and unexploded ordnance is still found), and remember that many places are graves. This guide tries to present both sides' dead and both sides' crimes plainly and without glorification."}],"orig":[{"h":"14 April 1931: the Second Republic","t":"After municipal elections in which Republican candidates swept the cities, King Alfonso XIII left Spain and the Second Republic was proclaimed on 14 April 1931. The new Cortes, dominated by Republicans and Socialists, passed a democratic constitution in December 1931 that separated Church and state, gave women the vote and allowed regional autonomy; Catalonia obtained its Statute in 1932. Under Prime Minister Manuel Azaña the government attempted army reform, agrarian reform in the great estates of the south, secular schooling and restrictions on the religious orders. The reforms raised huge hopes among landless labourers and workers, and deep alarm among landowners, much of the officer corps and Catholic Spain."},{"h":"1932–1933: pressure from both flanks","t":"The Republic was attacked from right and left. In August 1932 General José Sanjurjo's coup attempt in Seville (the Sanjurjada) collapsed within a day. Anarchist insurrections broke out in 1932 and 1933; at Casas Viejas (Cádiz) in January 1933 Assault Guards killed villagers after a rising, damaging Azaña's government. The Catholic right organised in the mass party CEDA under José María Gil Robles, while José Antonio Primo de Rivera founded the Falange in October 1933. In the November 1933 elections — the first in which women voted — the centre-right won, and a Radical Party government under Alejandro Lerroux, dependent on CEDA votes, began to slow or reverse the reforms."},{"h":"October 1934: Asturias and Catalonia","t":"When three CEDA ministers entered the government in October 1934, the left called a revolutionary general strike. It failed in most of Spain, but in Catalonia President Lluís Companys proclaimed a 'Catalan State within a Spanish Federal Republic' on 6 October; it lasted about ten hours. In Asturias, miners of the UGT, CNT and Communists seized much of the region for two weeks (5–19 October). The government, with General Franco coordinating from Madrid, sent in the Legion and Moroccan Regulares. Hugh Thomas estimated about 2,000 deaths, including those killed in the repression, and 15,000–30,000 were arrested. Each side drew its own lesson: for the left, fascism was at the gates; for the right, revolution."},{"h":"February 1936: the Popular Front","t":"Scandals brought down the Radical-CEDA governments, and elections were called for 16 February 1936. The left united in the Popular Front (Republicans, Socialists, Communists, POUM, with anarchist voters largely turning out), against a right-wing coalition. The Popular Front won 267 seats to the right's 132 on a narrow popular-vote margin; the fairness of the count in some provinces is still argued over by historians (Villa García and Álvarez Tardío, 2017, versus their critics). Azaña formed a government, amnestied the 1934 prisoners and restored Catalan autonomy; in May he became President and Santiago Casares Quiroga Prime Minister. Land occupations, strikes, church burnings and street killings between left and right militants followed."},{"h":"Spring 1936: the plot","t":"Army officers had begun conspiring soon after the election. The government moved suspect generals away from Madrid — Franco to the Canary Islands, Mola to Pamplona, Goded to the Balearics — which in practice let them build networks in the provinces. Mola, as 'the Director', drafted instructions calling for extreme violence to crush resistance. Sanjurjo, in exile in Portugal, was to lead the new regime. Monarchist and Carlist leaders pledged support; the Carlists offered thousands of armed Requetés in Navarre. The Falange, banned and its leader José Antonio imprisoned since March, joined the plot. Franco hesitated until late; a British-registered aircraft, the Dragon Rapide, was chartered in London to fly him from the Canaries to Morocco."},{"h":"12–13 July 1936: two killings","t":"On 12 July 1936 gunmen of the right shot dead Lieutenant José Castillo of the Assault Guards, a known Socialist, in Madrid. In retaliation, in the early hours of 13 July a group of Assault Guards and Socialist militants seized the monarchist leader José Calvo Sotelo from his home and murdered him; his body was left at the East Cemetery. That men connected to the state's own police had killed a leading opposition deputy shocked conservative Spain and removed the last hesitations among some conspirators. Mola fixed the date of the rising for 17–18 July."},{"h":"17–18 July 1936: the coup","t":"Warned that the plot had been discovered, the garrison in Melilla rose on the afternoon of 17 July; Ceuta and Tetuán followed and Spanish Morocco was in rebel hands by the next morning. On 18 July Franco proclaimed a state of war in Las Palmas and flew to Morocco; Queipo de Llano seized Seville; risings followed across the peninsula. Casares Quiroga refused to arm the unions and resigned; a compromise cabinet under Martínez Barrio lasted hours; on 19 July José Giral's government distributed arms to the workers' organisations. Where loyal Assault and Civil Guards joined armed workers — Madrid, Barcelona, Valencia — the coup failed. Where the army and Civil Guard held together, it succeeded. Spain was split in two."}],"factors":[{"h":"Non-intervention","t":"Fearing a general European war, Britain and France sponsored a Non-Intervention Agreement in August 1936, overseen by a committee in London from September. Germany, Italy and the USSR signed it and broke it. In practice it denied the legal government of Spain the right to buy arms openly, while Germany and Italy supplied the rebels on a large scale. France closed its border for much of the war. Historians broadly agree that non-intervention worked against the Republic, which was pushed into dependence on the Soviet Union."},{"h":"Foreign aid on both sides","t":"The rebels received German aircraft, tanks, artillery and the Condor Legion (about 16,000 Germans served over time), and Italian troops, aircraft and arms on a larger scale (Italy's CTV numbered up to about 50,000 at a time), with Portuguese help and volunteers. The Republic's main supplier was the USSR, which sent aircraft, T-26 tanks, artillery and some 3,000 advisers and technicians, paid for with Spain's gold reserve; Mexico sent rifles. International Brigade volunteers added tens of thousands of fighters. Axis aid was steadier and was easier to deliver than Soviet shipments across the Mediterranean."},{"h":"The Army of Africa and the airlift","t":"The rebels' best troops — the Legion and the Moroccan Regulares — were stranded in Morocco when the Republican fleet, whose crews had largely mutinied against their officers, blocked the Strait. From late July 1936 German Junkers Ju 52s and Italian Savoia-Marchetti SM.81s flew thousands of men to Seville, the first large-scale military airlift. These professional soldiers drove north through Extremadura to the outskirts of Madrid in three months, defeating militias in open country and carrying out mass killings along the way."},{"h":"Divisions inside the Republic","t":"The Republic combined a liberal-democratic government with a social revolution that broke out in July 1936 in Catalonia, Aragon and elsewhere: collectivised land and factories, militia rule and anticlerical violence. Anarchists and the POUM wanted to push the revolution forward; Communists, Socialists and Republicans wanted a centralised state and army first. The conflict erupted in the Barcelona May Days of 1937 and ended in the suppression of the POUM, and again in Casado's coup against Negrín in March 1939. The Nationalists, by contrast, were unified under one command and one party from 1937."},{"h":"Air power and the bombing of cities","t":"Spain was a testing ground for air war. German and Italian aircraft, and in 1936–37 Soviet fighters and bombers on the Republican side, fought for air superiority, which the Nationalists held from 1938. Madrid was bombed and shelled from November 1936; Durango and Gernika were destroyed in 1937; Italian aircraft from Mallorca raided Barcelona, Valencia, Alicante and other coastal cities. Republican aircraft also bombed Nationalist-held cities such as Córdoba, Valladolid and Palma. The response — hundreds of public air-raid shelters built by municipalities and neighbours — is one of the war's most visitable legacies."},{"h":"Holding Madrid and the problem of logistics","t":"The failure to take Madrid in November 1936 turned a coup into a long war. Soviet tanks and aircraft, the International Brigades, a disciplined Communist-led militia and the city's mass mobilisation stopped the assault; the Nationalists dug in at the Ciudad Universitaria and Casa de Campo for over two years. The Republic's offensives from 1937 won surprise but failed on supply, reserves and command: it could not replace equipment losses, while the Nationalists held the food-producing interior, the northern industry after 1937, and steadier foreign supply."},{"h":"Repression, refugees and exile","t":"Both zones saw mass killing of perceived enemies, especially in the first months: in the Republican zone clergy, landowners, right-wing politicians and suspected rebels; in the Nationalist zone left-wing activists, trade unionists, officials and teachers, often through systematic military orders. After victory the Franco regime continued executions, camps and prison labour for years. Refugee flows marked the war: the Desbandá from Málaga in 1937, and the Retirada of nearly half a million people into France in 1939. Many exiles stayed in France, Mexico, the USSR and Latin America; some ended up in Nazi camps."}],"clock":[["17 JULY — AFTERNOON AND NIGHT"],["c. 17:00","Warned that their plans had been discovered, officers in Melilla rise early, seize the garrison and arrest the loyal commander, General Romerales.","Melilla","HIGH"],["Evening","The rising spreads to Ceuta and Tetuán; loyal officers and trade unionists are shot. Orwell served in its militia."],["ERC","Esquerra Republicana de Catalunya — Catalan left-republican party of Companys."],["PNV","Partido Nacionalista Vasco — the Catholic Basque Nationalist Party of Aguirre."],["CEDA","Confederación Española de Derechas Autónomas — the mass Catholic right-wing party of Gil Robles."],["Falange","Falange Española (de las JONS) — the fascist party founded by José Antonio Primo de Rivera in 1933; its militia grew hugely after July 1936."],["FET y de las JONS","The single party created by Franco's unification decree of April 1937, merging Falangists and Carlists."],["Carlists","Traditionalist monarchists, strongest in Navarre, loyal to a rival branch of the Bourbons; fervently Catholic."],["Requetés","The Carlist militia, recognisable by their red berets; a key rebel force in 1936."],["Regulares","Moroccan infantry and cavalry units of the Spanish army under Spanish officers, part of the Army of Africa."],["Legión","The Spanish Foreign Legion (Tercio), created in 1920: shock troops of the Army of Africa. José Millán-Astray founded it; Franco was his second-in-command and led its 1st Bandera."],["Guardia Civil","The rural paramilitary police; it split in 1936, and its choice often decided who won a town."],["Guardia de Asalto","The Republic's urban Assault Guard, created in 1931; mostly loyal in July 1936."],["Checa","Unofficial detention and interrogation centre run by parties, unions or police in the Republican zone, especially in 1936."],["Paseo","'Taking for a ride' — extra-judicial killing of a detainee, usually at night on a roadside or cemetery wall, in both zones in 1936."],["Saca","The removal of prisoners from a jail to be killed, as at Paracuellos in 1936 and in Nationalist-zone jails."],["Brigadas Internacionales","The International Brigades of foreign volunteers in the Republican army, 1936–38."],["Legión Cóndor","The German air and ground unit sent by Hitler to support Franco, 1936–39."],["CTV","Corpo Truppe Volontarie — Mussolini's expeditionary corps of Italian troops in Spain."],["Ejército Popular","The Republic's Popular Army, formed from late 1936 from the militias and loyal units, organised in mixed brigades."],["Sublevados","'Those who rose' — neutral term for the rebels of July 1936."],["Nacionales","The rebels' own name for their side; 'Nationalists' in English."],["Rojos","'Reds' — period Nationalist term for all supporters of
